is indecomposable and non-projective, it is also indecomposable in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. Thus
H_{*}^{0}(M_{i})
is indecomposable in
\mathscr{C}
.

(3)
Let
X_{0}:={\bf R}(X[-1])[1]
. Then
X_{0}[-1]\in\mathscr{X}
and there is a triangle
X_{0}\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{X}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}_{*}(X)\to X_{0}[1]
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
by Lemma
4.6
(1). Thus
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X_{0}[n])=0
for all
n\geq 0
by Proposition
4.1
. In particular,
{\rm Ext}_{A}^{1}(M,X_{0})=0
.
Since both
X
and
H^{0}_{*}(X)
lie in
\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S}
, we see that
X_{0}\in\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S}
and the above triangle induces a triangle

X_{0}\to X\to H^{0}_{*}(X)\to\Omega_{M}^{-}(X_{0})
in
\mathscr{C}
by Lemma
3.16
(2). Further,
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X_{0}[-2])=0
by Lemma
4.2
(1),
and
X_{0}[-1]=\Omega_{A}(X_{0})\simeq\Omega_{M}(X_{0})\in\mathscr{G}
by
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X_{0})=0
. Thus
X_{0}[-1]\in\mathscr{H}
by Proposition
4.1
.
Suppose that
X
is in
\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S}
. Then
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[1],X_{0})\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X_{0}[-1])\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,{\bf R}(X[-1]))\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X[-1])\in\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
, where the last isomorphism follows from the fact that
{\bf R}:A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}\to\mathscr{X}
is the right adjoint of the inclusion

\mathscr{X}\subseteq A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. Now, it follows from
X_{0}\in\mathscr{G}
and
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X_{0})=0
that
X_{0}\in\mathscr{E}
. Thus
X_{0}\in\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S}
.
By Lemma
4.4
(2), both
\Omega_{M}(X_{0})
and
H^{0}_{*}(X)
belong to
\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}
.
\square

Corollary 4.9
.

The module
{{}_{A}}M
is projective if and only if
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=0
if and only if
(\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=0
.

Proof.
If
{{}_{A}}M
is projective, then
\mathscr{S}
consists of projective
A
-modules by Corollary
3.13
, which implies
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=0=(\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
.
If
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=0
, then
\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-mod}}=0
by Corollary
4.8
(1), that is,
\Gamma=0
, and therefore
{{}_{A}}M
is projective.
\square

Lemma 4.10
.

The object
N
has a complete
{\rm Add}(M)
-resolution:

\cdots\longrightarrow M^{-3}\longrightarrow M^{-2}\longrightarrow P^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\longrightarrow}}M\oplus P^{0}\longrightarrow P^{1}\longrightarrow M^{2}\longrightarrow M^{3}\longrightarrow\cdots

satisfying

(1)

N={\rm Coker}(\partial)
,
M^{i}\in{\rm Add}(M)
for all
|i|\geq 2
and
P^{i}\in{\rm Add}(A)
for all
|i|\leq 1
; and

(2)

{\rm Ker}(\partial)\in\mathscr{H}
and
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,{\rm Ker}(\partial))\simeq D\big({\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\nu_{A}(M))\big)
as
\Gamma
-modules.

Proof.

(1)
Let
\tau_{M}:M\to N
be the morphism in
A
-
Mod
in Lemma
4.6
(1). Up to projective direct summand, we may assume that
\tau_{M}
is a homomorphism in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
and a preimage of the
\tau_{M}
in
A
-
Mod
. Let
f:P^{0}\to{\rm Coker}(\tau_{M})
be a projective cover of
{\rm Coker}(\tau_{M})
. Then there exists a homomorphism
f_{0}:P^{0}\to N
such that
f
is the composition of
f_{0}
with the quotient map
N\to{\rm Coker}(\tau_{M})
. Moreover, the map
\pi_{0}:=(\tau_{M},f_{0}):M\oplus P^{0}\to N
is a right
{\rm Add}(M)
-approximation of
N
. Let
K:={\rm Ker}(\pi_{0})
with a projective cover
\pi_{1}:P^{-1}\to K
.
Then
K\simeq\Omega_{M}(N)
in
\mathscr{D}
. By the proof of Corollary
4.8
(3), we see that
K\in\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S}
,
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,K)=0
and

K[-1]=\Omega_{A}(K)\simeq\Omega_{M}(K)\in\mathscr{H}
. Consequently,
\pi_{1}
is a right
{\rm Add}(M)
-approximation of
K
. Now, let
\partial:P^{-1}\to M\oplus P^{0}
be the composition of
\pi_{1}
with the inclusion
\lambda_{1}:K\to M\oplus P^{0}
. Then
{\rm Coker}(\partial)=N
and
{\rm Ker}(\partial)\simeq K[-1]
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. Since
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,M)=0
and
{\rm Add}(M)={\rm Prod}(M)
, a minimal injective envelope
N\to P^{1}
is a left
{\rm Add}(M)
-approximation of
N
. Thus
(1)
holds.

(2)
Thanks to
N\in\mathscr{H}
, there holds
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,N[-1])=0={\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,N[-2])
by Proposition
4.1
. This implies that

{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\lambda_{1}[-1]):{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,K[-1])\to{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M[-1])
is an isomorphism. By
(\lozenge)
,

{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M[-1])\simeq D\big({\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[-1],\nu_{A}(M)[-1])\big)\simeq D\big({\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\nu_{A}(M))\big)

as
\Gamma
-modules.
Thus
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,{\rm Ker}(\partial)\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,K[-1])\simeq D({\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\nu_{A}(M)))
.
\square

Corollary 4.11
.

If
A
is a symmetric algebra and
\Gamma
is a Frobenius algebra, then
\Omega_{M}^{2}(N)\simeq N
in
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Since
A
is symmetric,
\nu_{A}(M)\simeq M
as
A
-
\Lambda
-bimodules. By Lemma
4.10
(2),
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,{\rm Ker}(\partial))\simeq D\big({\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M)\big)=D(\Gamma)
as
\Gamma
-modules. Note that
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,N)\simeq\Gamma
by Lemmas
4.6
(2) and
4.7
(2). Since
\Gamma
is a Frobenius algebra,
\Gamma\simeq D(\Gamma)
as
\Gamma
-modules. Moreover,
{\rm Ker}(\partial)\in\mathscr{H}
and
{\rm Ker}(\partial)\simeq\Omega_{M}^{2}(N)
in
\mathscr{C}

by Lemma
4.10
. It follows from Lemmas
4.6
(2) and
4.7
that
{\rm Ker}(\partial)\simeq N
in
\mathscr{H}
. Thus
\Omega_{M}^{2}(N)\simeq N
in
\mathscr{C}
.
\square

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
X\in{{}^{\bot>0}}M
and
l_{X}:X\to M^{X}
be a left
{\rm Add}(M)
-approximation of
X
. Then
there is an exact sequence of
\Gamma
-modules:

0\longrightarrow{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\tau_{M})_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(l_{X})^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M^{X}).

Proof.
Keep all notations introduced in the proof of Lemma
4.10
. Recall that
{\rm Prod}(M)={\rm Add}(M)
and
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,M)=0
. If
M_{0}\in{\rm Add}(M)
, then
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,M_{0})=0
. It follows from
M^{X}\in{\rm Add}(M)
that
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,M^{X})=0
. This implies that the composition of
(\tau_{M})_{*}
with
(l_{X})^{*}
is
0
. Thus

{\rm Im}((\tau_{M})_{*})\subseteq{\rm Ker}((l_{X})^{*})
.

Applying
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(-,X)
to the triangle
K\to M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}N\to K[1]
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
yields an exact sequence

{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(K[1],X)\to{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\tau_{M})_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X)
. By
(\lozenge)
, we have
\underline{{{}^{\bot>0}}M}=\underline{M}^{\bot\leq-2}=\mathscr{Y}[2]
. Moreover,
\underline{K}[-1]\in\mathscr{H}\subseteq\mathscr{X}
by the proof of Lemma
4.10
. Thus
\underline{K}[1]\in\mathscr{X}[2]
.
Since
X\in\underline{{{}^{\bot>0}}M}
and
(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
is a torsion pair in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
, we have
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(K[1],X)=0
.
Thus
(\tau_{M})_{*}
is injective. It remains to show
{\rm Ker}((l_{X})^{*})\subseteq{\rm Im}((\tau_{M})_{*})
.

Suppose that
g\in{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X)={\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M\oplus P_{0},X)
with
gl_{X}=0
. Let

0\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{A}^{-}(X)\to 0
be a short exact
sequence of
A
-modules in which
\lambda_{0}
is an injective envelope. We claim that the pair
(g\lambda_{0},0)
with
g\lambda_{0}:M\oplus P^{0}\to Q^{0}
and
0:N\to\Omega_{A}^{-}(X)
can be completed into the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{-3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{-3}}
\textstyle{M^{-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{-2}}
\textstyle{P^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{f^{-1}}
\textstyle{M\oplus P^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{g\lambda_{0}}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{-3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{0}}
\textstyle{\Omega_{A}^{-}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

in which the first arrow is an
{\rm Add}(M)
-resolution of
N
(see Lemma
4.10
) and the second arrow is a minimal projective resolution of
\Omega_{A}^{-}(X)
. In other words, there is a chain map
f^{\bullet}:=(\cdots,f^{-3},f^{-2},f^{-1},g\lambda_{0},0).

To show the existence of the chain map, one splits the long exact sequences into a series of short exact sequences, and then construct relevant homomorphisms together with commutative diagrams between these sequences. We carry out the details as follows.

For
K:={\rm Ker}(\pi_{0})
, let
\lambda_{1}:K\to M\oplus P^{0}
be the canonical inclusion, and let
\alpha:K\to X
be the composition of
\lambda_{1}
with
g:M\oplus P^{0}\to X
. Since
P^{-1}
is projective, there exists a map
f^{-1}:P^{-1}\to Q^{-1}
making the diagram commute:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{A}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{A}(\alpha)}
\textstyle{P^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{-1}}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{A}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{1}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

To construct
f^{-2}
, we will show that
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\alpha[-1]):{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,K[-1])\to{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X[-1])

is zero, where
[-1]
denotes
\Omega_{A}
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
.

By the proof of Lemma
4.10
(2),
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\lambda_{1}[-1])
is an isomorphism. It is enough to show that the map
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,g[-1]):{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M[-1])\to{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X[-1])
is zero. Applying
D:\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}\to\Gamma^{\rm op}\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
to this map, we see that
(\lozenge)

yields
D{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,g[-1])\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(g,\nu_{A}(M)):{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(X,\nu_{A}(M))\to{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\nu_{A}(M))
.
Although
D
may not be an equivalence in general, it is always exact and reflects zero objects. Thus
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,g[-1])=0
if and only if
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(g,\nu_{A}(M))=0
. Since
{\rm add}(\nu_{A}(M))={\rm add}(M)={\rm Prod}(M)
,
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(g,\nu_{A}(M))=0
is equivalent to saying that
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(g,M^{\prime}):{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(X,M^{\prime})\to{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M^{\prime})
is
0
for any
M^{\prime}\in{\rm Prod}(M)
. By assumption,
l_{X}
is a left
{\rm Add}(M)
-approximation of
X
and

gl_{X}=0
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. This leads to
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(g,M^{\prime})=0
. Thus
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\alpha[-1])=0
, and therefore

{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M^{-2},\alpha[-1])=0
, due to
M^{-2}\in{\rm Add}(M)
. Hence there are two homomorphisms
f^{-2}
and
\beta
such that the diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{M}(\Omega_{A}(K))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{M^{-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{-2}}
\textstyle{\Omega_{A}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{A}(\alpha)}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{A}^{2}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{A}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since
X\in\underline{{{}^{\bot>0}}M}=\underline{M}^{\bot\leq-2}
, we have
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\Omega_{A}^{n}(X))={\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X[-n])=0
for all
n\geq 2
. Then
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M^{-n-1},\Omega_{A}^{n}(X))=0
, due to
M^{-n-1}\in{\rm Add}(M)
. Consequently, the components
f^{-n-1}:M^{-n-1}\to Q^{-n-1}
for
n\geq 2
in
f^{\bullet}
can be constructed.

Since
N\in\mathscr{H}\subseteq\underline{M}^{\bot>0}\cap\underline{\mathscr{S}}
by Proposition
4.1
, it follows from Proposition
3.8
(1) that
f^{\bullet}=0
in
{\mathscr{K}}(A)
. Therefore there are homomorphisms
h:N\to Q^{0}
and
s^{0}:M\oplus P^{0}\to Q^{-1}
such that
g\lambda_{0}=s^{0}\mu_{1}\lambda_{0}+\pi_{0}h
and
h\mu_{0}=0
. Since
\lambda_{0}
is the kernel of
\mu_{0}
, there is a unique map
h_{0}:N\to X
satisfying
h=h_{0}\lambda_{0}
. Since
\lambda_{0}
is injective,
g=s^{0}\mu_{1}+\pi_{0}h_{0}
. This forces
g=\tau_{M}h_{0}
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
and shows
g\in{\rm Im}((\tau_{M})_{*})
.
Thus
{\rm Ker}((l_{X})^{*})\subseteq{\rm Im}((\tau_{M})_{*})
. Finally, via the isomorphism
\Gamma\simeq{\rm\underline{End}}_{A}(N)
in Lemma
4.7
(2),
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,X)
becomes a
\Gamma
-module and
(\tau_{M})_{*}
is a homomorphism of
\Gamma
-modules.
\square

Corollary 4.13
.

The object
N
is compact in
\underline{{{}^{\bot>0}}M}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,-):\underline{{{}^{\bot>0}}M}\to\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
commutes with direct sums. Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a set of
A
-modules in
{{}^{\bot>0}}M
with
I
an index set. For each
i
, let
g_{i}:X_{i}\to M_{i}
be a left
{\rm Add}(M)
-approximation of
X_{i}
. According to
{\rm Add}(M)={\rm Prod}(M)
, the direct sum
g=(g_{i})_{i\in I}:\bigoplus_{i\in I}X_{i}\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}M_{i}
of all these
g_{i}
is a left
{\rm Add}(M)
-approximation of
\bigoplus_{i\in I}X_{i}
. Note that
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,-):A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}\to\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
commutes with direct sums since
M
is compact in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. By Lemma
4.12
, we can construct the following commutative diagram with exact arrows and canonical vertical maps:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,X_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,\bigoplus_{i\in I}X_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\bigoplus_{i\in I}X_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\bigoplus_{i\in I}M_{i}).}

Thus the first vertical map is an isomorphism.
\square

Theorem 4.14
.

(1)
The object
N
belongs to
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
and is compact in
({{}^{\bot>0}}M)/[M]
. In particular,
N
is compact in
\mathscr{C}
.

(2)
For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists a natural isomorphism of homological functors:

H^{n}\big({\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M^{\bullet}_{-})\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{M}\big(\Omega_{M}^{n}(N),-\big):\;\;\mathscr{C}\longrightarrow\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}.

Proof

(1)
By Lemma
4.2
,
N\in(\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
. Note that
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[1],N)=0
by Proposition
4.1

and
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,N)\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M)
by Lemma
4.7
(1). This shows
N\in\mathscr{E}
. Since
{{}^{\bot>0}}M
contains
{\rm Add}(M)
and is closed under direct sums in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
, we see that
{{}^{\bot>0}}M
, as an additive category, has coproducts. Moreover, by Lemma
2.1
(2), the quotient functor
\underline{{{}^{\bot>0}}M}\to({{}^{\bot>0}}M)/[M]
preserves coproducts and compact objects. Now
(1)
follows from Corollary
4.13
.

(2)

Let
M^{\bullet}_{X}:=(M_{X}^{n},d_{X}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a complete
{\rm Add}(M)
-resolution of
X
. Then
X\simeq{\rm Coker}(d_{X}^{-1})
and

{\rm Hom}_{A}(M,M^{\bullet}_{X})
is acyclic. Clearly, there is a canonical ring homomorphism
\Lambda\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma
and
the functor
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,-):A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}\to\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
is naturally isomorphic to the composition of
G
with

\Gamma\otimes_{\Lambda}-
. Then
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M^{\bullet}_{X})\simeq\Gamma\otimes_{\Lambda}{\rm Hom}_{A}(M,M^{\bullet}_{X})
as complexes.
Since
\Gamma\otimes_{\Lambda}-
is right exact, the sequence

{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M^{n-1}_{X})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d_{X}^{n-1})^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M^{n}_{X})\longrightarrow{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\Omega_{M}^{-n}(X))\longrightarrow 0

is exact for all
n
. As the inclusion
\lambda_{n}:\Omega_{M}^{-n}(X)\to M^{n+1}_{X}
is a left
{\rm Add}(M)
-approximation of
\Omega_{M}^{-n}(X)
,
it follows from Lemma
4.12
that the sequence

0\longrightarrow{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,\Omega_{M}^{-n}(X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\tau_{M})_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\Omega_{M}^{-n}(X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\lambda_{n})^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M_{X}^{n+1})

is exact. Consequently,
H^{n}({\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M^{\bullet}_{X}))\simeq H^{n}(\Gamma\otimes_{\Lambda}{\rm Hom}_{A}(M,M^{\bullet}_{X}))\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,\Omega_{M}^{-n}(X)).

Since
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,M^{\prime})=0
for any
M^{\prime}\in{\rm Add}(M)
, we have

{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,\Omega_{M}^{-n}(X))={\rm\underline{Hom}}_{M}(N,\Omega_{M}^{-n}(X))\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{M}(\Omega_{M}^{n}(N),X).

This shows
(2)
.

4.3
Compact objects from left approximations

In this subsection, we characterize compact objects in
\mathscr{C}
in terms of
M
-filtered modules (see Proposition
4.17
) and show that all objects of
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
are compact in
\mathscr{C}
. We then establish a connection between
(\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
and
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
by employing strong generators (see Corollary
4.18
).

As a preparation, we recall a construction of
{\bf L}(X)
and
{\bf R}(X)
from the proof of
[
13
, Theorem III.2.3]
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}:=\{\underline{M}[n]\mid n\geq 0\}\subseteq\mathscr{X}
. Denote by
{\rm Add}(\mathfrak{A})
the full subcategory of
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
consisting of direct summands of arbitrary direct sums of objects of
\mathfrak{A}
. For a full subcategory
\mathscr{U}
of
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
, we denote by
\mathscr{U}^{\star n}=\mathscr{U}\star\mathscr{U}\star\cdots\star\mathscr{U}
(
n
-factors) the category of
n
-extensions of
\mathscr{U}
by
\mathscr{U}
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
.

Let
X\in A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. We construct a right
{\rm Add}(\mathfrak{A})
-approximation
f_{1}:Q_{1}\to X
of
X
as follows:
Consider the set
I_{X}
of the union of
\underline{{\rm Hom}}_{A}(P,X)
with
P
running over
\mathfrak{A}
,
define
Q_{1}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in I_{X}}P_{\lambda}
and take
f_{1}
to be the morphism induced by
I_{X}
, where
\lambda
is morphism from
P_{\lambda}
to
X
. Then we extend
f_{1}
to a triangle
Q_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{1}\longrightarrow Q_{1}[1]
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
.
Now, we can repeat this construction by replacing
X
by
X_{1}\in A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. In general, for each
n\geq 0
, we can inductively construct a triangle

Q_{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{n+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{n+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{n+1}\longrightarrow Q_{n+1}[1]

in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
such that
f_{n+1}
is a right
{\rm Add}(\mathfrak{A})
-approximation of
X_{n}
with
X_{0}:=X
. Setting
T_{1}:=Q_{1}
and
h_{1}:=f_{1}
, we then construct inductively a tower of objects

T_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{3}\longrightarrow\cdots
which is embedded into the following tower of triangles in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
:

\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{1}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{2}}
\textstyle{T_{1}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{1}[1]}
\textstyle{(\star\star)}
\textstyle{T_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{2}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}g_{2}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{3}}
\textstyle{T_{2}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{2}[1]}
\textstyle{T_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{3}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}}
\textstyle{X_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{3}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

Applying the Octahedral Axiom of triangulated categories for each
n
yields a series of triangles

(\diamond\diamond)\quad T_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{n+1}\longrightarrow T_{n}[1].

This implies
T_{n}\in{\rm Add}(\mathfrak{A})^{\star n}\subseteq\mathscr{X}
for
n\geq 1
.

Let
\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(T_{n})
be the
homotopy colimit

in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
of the tower of objects

T_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{3}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\longrightarrow T_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{n+1}\longrightarrow\cdots

defined by the triangle

(\sharp)\quad\quad\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1-\tau_{*})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}\longrightarrow\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(T_{n})\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}[1]

where the morphism
(1-\tau_{*})
is induced by
({\rm Id}_{T_{n}},-\tau_{n}):T_{n}\to T_{n}\oplus T_{n+1\hookrightarrow}\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}
.
Now, we choose
\tau_{n}
as a representative in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
and denote by
\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow}T_{n}
the colimit of the direct system
\{(T_{n},\tau_{n})\mid n\geq 1\}
of
A
-modules.
Then there is a short exact sequence

0\to\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1-\tau_{*})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}\longrightarrow\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow}T_{n}\longrightarrow 0

which induces a canonical triangle in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
:

\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1-\tau_{*})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}\longrightarrow\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow}T_{n}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}[1].

This implies that
\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(T_{n})\simeq\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow}T_{n}
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
.
Since the homotopy colimit of a tower of triangles in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
is a triangle (for example, see
[
35
, Lemma 4.2]
), there is a triangle in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
:

({\ddagger})\quad\quad\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(T_{n})\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(X_{n})\longrightarrow\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(T_{n})[1].

By the proof of
[
13
, Theorem III.2.3 and Remark III.2.7]
, we can show
\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(T_{n})\in\mathscr{X}
and
\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(X_{n})\in\mathscr{Y}
.
Since
(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
is a torsion pair in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
, there are isomorphisms

{\bf R}(X)\simeq\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(T_{n})\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;{\bf L}(X)\simeq\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(X_{n}).

Recall that
T:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}
stands for the left adjoint of the inclusion
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
(see Lemma
3.20
(3)) and
N
is defined to be
H_{*}^{0}(M)
.

Lemma 4.15
.

(1)
If
X\in{{}^{\bot>0}M}
, then
{\bf R}(X)
lies in
\underline{\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S}}
and is isomorphic in
\mathscr{C}
to
T(T_{3})
.

(2)
If
X\in\mathscr{G}
, then
{\bf L}(X)\in\underline{M}^{\bot}
. If further
X\in\mathscr{E}
, then

T_{3}\in A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}
.

(3)

\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)\simeq\Omega_{A}^{-}(N)\simeq T(\Omega_{A}^{-}(M))
in
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

(1)
Recall from Section
4.2
that
\mathscr{X}=\underline{M}^{\bot>0}\cap\underline{\mathscr{S}}
and
\mathscr{Y}={{}^{\bot\geq-1}}\underline{M}\subseteq{{}^{\bot>0}}\underline{M}=\underline{{}^{\bot>0}M}
. Let
X\in{{}^{\bot>0}M}
. Since there is a triangle
{\bf R}(X)\to X\to{\bf L}(X)\to{\bf R}(X)[1]
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
with
{\bf R}(X)\in\mathscr{X}
and
{\bf L}(X)\in\mathscr{Y}
, we see that
{\bf R}(X)
lies in
\underline{{}^{\bot>0}M}
, and therefore in
\underline{\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S}}
.

Set
\Lambda_{n}:=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[i],X_{n})\neq 0\}
for
n\geq 0
. Since
{\rm add}({{}_{A}}M)={\rm add}(\nu_{A}(M))
, it is clear that
\underline{{{}^{\bot>0}}M}=\underline{M}^{\bot\leq-2}
by
(\lozenge)
. This implies
\Lambda_{0}\subseteq\{0,1\}
. So we can choose
Q_{1}=M^{(I_{1,0})}\oplus M[1]^{(I_{1,1})}
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
for some index sets
I_{1,0}
and
I_{1,1}
. For a natural number
n
, we apply
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[i],-)
for
i\geq 0
to the triangle

Q_{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{n+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{n+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{n+1}\longrightarrow Q_{n+1}[1]

in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}.
This yields an exact sequence of abelian groups:

{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[i],Q_{n+1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f_{n+1})^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[i],X_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(g_{n+1})^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[i],X_{n+1})\longrightarrow{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[i],Q_{n+1}[1]).

Since
f_{n+1}
is a right
{\rm Add}(\mathfrak{A})
-approximation of
X_{n}
, the map
(f_{n+1})^{*}
is always surjective, and therefore there is an injection

{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[i],X_{n+1})\hookrightarrow{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[i],Q_{n+1}[1])
for
i\geq 0
. Using the fact
{\rm Add}(\underline{M})\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{G}}=\underline{M}^{\bot\neq 0,-1}
, we then can show
\Lambda_{n}\subseteq\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid n\leq i\leq 2n+1\}
by induction on
n
and choose
Q_{n+1}=\bigoplus_{j\in\Lambda_{n}}M[j]^{(I_{n+1,j})}
for some index sets
I_{n+1,j}
.

Consider
n\geq 3
. Then
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(Q_{n+1},M[1])=0
. It follows from (
\diamond\diamond
) and Lemma
3.16
(2) that there is a triangle
T_{n}\to T_{n+1}\to Q_{n+1}\to\Omega_{M}^{-}(T_{n})
in
\mathscr{D}
. We apply the functor
T
to this triangle and produce another triangle
T(T_{n})\to T(T_{n+1})\to T(Q_{n+1})\to\Omega_{M}^{-}(T(T_{n}))
in
\mathscr{C}
. Since
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[m],\mathscr{G})=0
for any
m\geq 2
, the minimal injective envelope
M[m]\to I^{m}
of
M[i]
is a left
\mathscr{G}
-approximation of
M[m]
. This means
T(M[m])\simeq I^{m}=0
in
\mathscr{C}
for any
m\geq 2
. Since
T
commutes with direct sums, we have
T(Q_{n+1})\simeq\bigoplus_{j\in\Lambda_{n}}T(M[j])^{(I_{n+1,j})}=0
. Thus
T(T_{n})\simeq T(T_{n+1})
. Consequently, the homotopy colimit
\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(T(T_{n}))
in
\mathscr{C}
of
\{(T(T_{n}),T(\tau_{n})\mid n\geq 1\}
is isomorphic to
T(T_{3})
. Moreover, due to
{\bf R}(X)\in{{}^{\bot 1}M}
, we see from Lemma
3.16
(2) that
{\bf R}(X)
is also the homotopy colimit in
\mathscr{D}
of
\{T_{n}\}
. Since
{\bf R}(X)\in\mathscr{G}
and
T
commutes with homotopy colimits by Lemma
3.20
(3), there are isomorphisms
{\bf R}(X)\simeq T({\bf R}(X))\simeq\underrightarrow{{\rm Hocolim}}(T(T_{n}))\simeq T(T_{3})
in
\mathscr{C}
.

(2)
If
X\in\mathscr{G}
, then
{\bf L}(X)\in\underline{M}^{\bot>0}
. This implies
{\bf L}(X)\in\underline{M}^{\bot}
because
{\bf L}(X)\in\mathscr{Y}=\underline{M}^{\bot\leq 0}
.
Let

\mathscr{A}:=\{U\in A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}\mid\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[n],U)\in\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-mod}}\}.

Since
\Gamma
is an Artin algebra
over a commutative Artin ring
R
, a
\Gamma
-module
N
is finitely generated if and only if
N
is a finitely generated
R
-module.
This implies that an
A
-module
U
lies in
\mathscr{A}
if and only if
\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[n],U)\in R\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
. The latter is
equivalent to saying that there is a non-negative integer
\delta_{U}
such that
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[n],U)=0
for
n>\delta_{U}
and
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[n],U)\in R\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
for
0\leq n\leq\delta_{U}
. Moreover,
\mathscr{A}
is closed under direct summands, finite direct sums, the shift
[-1]
and extensions of triangles in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. Now, it follows from
M\in\underline{M}^{\bot\neq 0,-1}
and
{\rm Hom}_{A}(M[1],M)\simeq D{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,\nu_{A}(M))
that
M[j]\in\mathscr{A}
for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Consequently, for
U\in\mathscr{A}
, there is a right
{\rm Add}(\mathfrak{A})
-approximation
f_{U}:Q_{U}\to U
of
U
such that
Q_{U}\in{\rm add}(\bigoplus_{n=0}^{m}M[n])
for some
m\geq 0
and the third term
C_{U}

of the triangle
Q_{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{U}}}{{\longrightarrow}}U\to C_{U}\to Q_{U}[1]
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
still lies in
\mathscr{A}
.

By Lemma
4.2
, there holds
\underline{\mathscr{E}}\subseteq\mathscr{A}
. If
X\in\mathscr{E}
, then
I_{n+1,j}
can be chosen to be finite sets and
Q_{n+1}\in{\rm add}(\bigoplus_{j\in\Lambda_{n}}M[j])\subseteq A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}
.

(3)
In the proof of
(1)
, we take
X=N
. Then
X\in\mathscr{E}
by Theorem
4.14
(1). Recall that
X\in\mathscr{H}\subseteq\underline{M}^{\bot\neq 0}
by Proposition
4.1
, and
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M)\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X)
by Lemma
4.7
(1). Further, we even have

(a)

\Lambda_{0}\subseteq\{0\}
,
Q_{1}=M
and
X_{1}[-2]\in\mathscr{H}
(see the proof of Lemma
4.10
); and

(b)

\Lambda_{n}\subseteq\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid n+1\leq i\leq 2n\}
and
Q_{n+1}\in{\rm add}(\bigoplus_{i=n+1}^{2n}M[i])\in A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}
for
n\geq 1
.

Since
M
lies in
\underline{\mathscr{G}}=\underline{M}^{\bot\neq 0,-1}
, there holds
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(Q_{3},M[1])=0
. By Lemma
3.16
(2), there exists a right triangle
T_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{3}\rightarrow\Omega_{M}^{-}(T_{2})
in
\mathscr{D}
. This gives rise to another triangle
T(T_{2})\to T(T_{3})\to T(Q_{3})\to\Omega_{M}^{-}(T(T_{2}))
in
\mathscr{C}
. Clearly,
T(Q_{3})=0
by
Q_{3}\in{\rm add}(M[3]\oplus M[4])
. Thus

T(T_{2})\simeq T(T_{3})
. It then follows from (1) and
X\in\mathscr{H}\subseteq\mathscr{X}
that
X\simeq{\bf R}(X)\simeq T(T_{2})
in
\mathscr{C}
.

By Lemma
4.10
,
X[1]=\Omega_{A}^{-}(X)\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}(X)
in
\mathscr{C}
. Further, we will show
T_{2}[1]=\Omega_{A}^{-}(T_{2})\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}(T_{2})
in
\mathscr{C}
. Actually, it suffices to prove that any homomorphism from
T_{2}
to a module in
{\rm Add}(M)
factorizes through the injective envelope of
T_{2}
, or equivalently,
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(T_{2},M)=0
. Thanks to
{\rm add}(M)={\rm add}(\nu_{A}(M))
, we will show
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(T_{2},\nu_{A}(M))=0
.

Indeed,
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(T_{2},\nu_{A}(M))\simeq D{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[1],T_{2})
by
(\lozenge)
. We apply
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[1],-)
to the triangle

X_{2}[-1]\to T_{2}\to X\to X_{2}
(see (
\star\star
)) and obtain an isomorphism
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[1],X_{2}[-1])\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[1],T_{2})
. Here we use the fact
X\in\mathscr{H}\subseteq\underline{M}^{\bot\neq 0}
. So, it is enough to show
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[1],X_{2}[-1])=0
. Recall that the triangle
Q_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{1}\to X_{2}\to Q_{2}[1]
has the following properties:
Q_{2}\in{\rm add}(M[2])
by
(b)
,
X_{1}\in\mathscr{H}[2]
by
(a)
, and there exists an injection
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[2],X_{2})\hookrightarrow{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[2],Q_{2}[1])
by the proof of
(1)
. It follows from
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,M[1])=0
that
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[1],X_{2}[-1])\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[2],X_{2})=0
. Thus
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[1],T_{2})=0
, and therefore
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(T_{2},\nu_{A}(M))=0
.

By the facts that
Q_{2}\in{\rm add}(M[2])
and
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M[2],M)=0
, we have

{\rm Ext}_{A}^{1}(Q_{2}[1],M)\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(Q_{2}[1],M[1])\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(Q_{2},M)=0.

By Lemma
3.16
(2), the triangle
M[1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{1}[1]}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{2}[1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{1}[1]}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{2}[1]\longrightarrow M[2]
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
can be extended to a right triangle
M[1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{1}[1]}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{2}[1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{1}[1]}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{2}[1]\longrightarrow\Omega_{M}^{-}(M[1])
in
\mathscr{D}
. This yields the triangle in
\mathscr{C}

T(M[1])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T(\tau_{1}[1])}}{{\longrightarrow}}T(T_{2}[1])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle T(\sigma_{1}[1])}}{{\longrightarrow}}T(Q_{2}[1])\longrightarrow\Omega_{M}^{-}(T(M[1]))

by Lemma
3.20
(3).
It then follows from
T(M[3])=0
that
T(Q_{2}[1])=0
and
T(M[1])\simeq T(T_{2}[1])
in
\mathscr{C}
. Moreover,
T(T_{2}[1])\simeq T(\Omega_{M}^{-}(T_{2}))\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}(T(T_{2}))\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}(X)
in
\mathscr{C}
, where the second isomorphism is due to the fact that
T
commutes with
\Omega_{M}^{-}
. Thus
T(\Omega_{A}^{-}(M))=T(M[1])\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}(X)
in
\mathscr{C}
.
\square

Now, we state a property of finitely
M
-filtered
A
-modules introduced in Definition
1.1
.

Lemma 4.16
.

If
X
is a finitely
M
-filtered
A
-module, then
X\in\mathscr{G}^{\bot>0}
and has an
{\rm add}(M)
-resolution
of finite length. Moreover, if the
A
-module
X
lies in
\mathscr{G}
, then
X\in{\rm add}(M)
.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{G}^{\bot>0}
contains
M
and is closed under cosyzygies and extensions in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
, we have
X\in\mathscr{G}^{\bot>0}
.
Now, let
\mathscr{M}
be the full subcategory of
A\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
consisting of all those modules having an
{\rm add}(M)
-resolution of finite length. Then
Y\in\mathscr{M}
if and only if
{\rm Hom}_{A}(M,Y)\in\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(\Lambda)
. Since
{{}_{A}}M
is a self-orthogonal generator,
Y\in\mathscr{M}
if and only if there is an exact sequence
0\to M_{n}\to M_{n-1}\to\cdots\to M_{0}\to Y\to 0
in
A\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
M_{j}\in{\rm add}(M)
for all
0\leq j\leq n
. Clearly,
\mathscr{M}\subseteq M^{\bot>0}\subseteq M^{\bot 1}
. As
\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(\Lambda)
is always closed under extensions in
\Lambda\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
,
\mathscr{M}
is closed under extensions in
A\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
. So, to show that the finitely
M
-filtered module
X
belongs to
\mathscr{M}
, it suffices to show
\Omega_{A}^{-i}(M)\in\mathscr{M}
for each
i\geq 0
. However, this follows from the exact sequence
0\to M\to I^{0}\to\cdots\to I^{i-1}\to\Omega_{A}^{-i}(M)\to 0
, where
I^{j}
is injective and therefore in
{\rm add}(M)
for
0\leq j\leq i-1
.
As to the last statement in the lemma, we notice
\mathscr{M}\cap{{}^{\bot>0}}M={\rm add}(M)
,
\mathscr{G}\subseteq{{}^{\bot>0}}M
and
\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{M}={\rm add}(M)
.
\square

In the following, we describe zero objects and compact objects in
\mathscr{C}
.
This is related to pure-projective modules. It is known that a module over an Artin algebra is
pure-projective
if and only if it is a direct sum of finitely generated, indecomposable modules.

Proposition 4.17
.

The following hold for
X\in\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S}
.

(1)

X
is compact in
\mathscr{C}
and isomorphic in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
to an
M
-filtered module.

(2)
If
X^{\bot 1}
is closed under direct sums of countably many finitely
M
-filtered
A
-modules in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
, then
X\in{\rm Add}(M)
.
In particular, if
X
is pure-projective, then
X\in{\rm Add}(M)
.

(3)
If
X\in\mathscr{H}\cap A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}
, then
X
is projective.

Proof.
Let
X\in\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S}
. We keep all notations in the proof of Lemmas
4.15
(1)-(2).

(1)
By Lemma
4.2
,
X\in\mathscr{X}
and
X\simeq{\bf R}(X)
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
.
Moreover, by Lemmas
4.15
(1)-(2),
{\bf R}(X)\simeq T(T_{3})
in
\mathscr{C}
with
T_{3}\in A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}
. Each object of
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}
is compact in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
and the quotient functor
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}\to\mathscr{D}
preserves compact objects by Lemma
2.1
(2). Hence
T_{3}
is compact in
\mathscr{D}
. As
T
preserves compact objects by Lemma
3.20
(3), both
T(T_{3})
and
X
are compact in
\mathscr{C}
.

It follows from
X\in\mathscr{E}
that the proofs of Lemmas
4.15
(1)-(2) yield
Q_{n+1}=\bigoplus_{j\in\Lambda_{n}}M[j]^{(I_{n+1,j})}
, where
\Lambda_{n}\subseteq\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid n\leq i\leq 2n+1\}
,
M[j]=\Omega_{A}^{-j}(M)
and
I_{n+1,j}
are finite sets. This implies
T_{n}\in A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}
for
n\geq 1
. Since triangles in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}
(up to isomorphism) are induced from short exact sequences, we can add finitely generated projective modules to
T_{n}
and assume that the associated map
\tau_{n}:T_{n}\to T_{n+1}
is injective and
{\rm Coker}(\tau_{n})
is isomorphic to a finite direct sum of modules in
\{A\}\cup\{\Omega_{A}^{-i}(M)\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Now, let
X^{\prime}
be the colimit of the direct system
\{(T_{n},\tau_{n})\mid n\geq 1\}
in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. Then the canonical map
T_{n}\to X^{\prime}
is injective. Thus
T_{n}
can be regarded as a submodule of
X^{\prime}
and
T_{n}\subseteq T_{n+1}
. Moreover,
X^{\prime}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}T_{n}
and is
M
-filtered. Now (1) follows from
X\simeq{\bf R}(X)\simeq X^{\prime}
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
.

(2)
Since all
T_{n}
are finitely
M
-filtered and
X\in\mathscr{G}
, we have
T_{n}\in X^{\bot 1}
by Lemma
4.16
. Let
Z:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}
.
The assumption of
(2)
implies
{\rm Ext}_{A}^{1}(X,Z)=0
. So the exact sequence

0\to Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1-\tau_{*})}}{{\longrightarrow}}Z\to X^{\prime}\to 0
induced by
\{\tau_{n}\mid n\geq 1\}
splits, and therefore
X^{\prime}
is isomorphic to a direct summand of

Z
. Since
T_{n}
is a finitely generated
A
-module, it is a direct sum of finitely many indecomposable submodules with the local endomorphism rings. It follows from
[
1
, Corollary 26.6]
that
X^{\prime}\simeq\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}T_{n}^{\prime}
, where
T_{n}^{\prime}
is a direct summand of
T_{n}
.
Moreover, by Lemma
4.16
,
T_{n}
has an
{\rm add}(M)
-resolution of finite length, and so does
T_{n}^{\prime}
. It follows from
X\simeq X^{\prime}
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
and
X\in\mathscr{G}
that
X^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}
, and therefore
T_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}
. Consequently,
T_{n}^{\prime}\in{\rm add}(M)
by the proof of Lemma
4.16
. Hence
X^{\prime}\in{\rm Add}(M)
and
X\in{\rm Add}(M)
.

For any
Z\in A\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
,
Z
is clearly finite presented. So
Z^{\bot 1}
is always closed under arbitrary direct sums in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. This implies that if
X
is pure-projective, then
X^{\bot 1}
is closed under arbitrary direct sums in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
, and therefore
X\in{\rm Add}(M)
.

(3)
By Lemmas
4.2
(1) and
4.4
(1),
\mathscr{H}\cap A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S}}
. It follows from
X\in A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{mod}}}}
that
X^{\bot 1}
is closed under arbitrary direct sums in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. Further,
X\in{\rm Add}(M)
by
(2)
, and
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,M)=0
by Lemma
4.10
. Due to
{\rm Add}(M)={\rm Prod}(M)
, we have
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,X)=0
. It follows from Lemma
4.7
(3) and
X\in\mathscr{H}
that
X\simeq 0
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. Thus
X
is projective.
\square

Now, we describe compact generators of the right-hand side in the recollement of Theorem
1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulated category. For a full subcategory
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{T}
, we denote by
\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle
the smallest full subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
containing
\mathcal{U}
and being closed under finite coproducts, direct summands and shifts.
Let
\mathcal{V}
be another full subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
. Define
\mathcal{U}\star\mathcal{V}
to be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
consisting of objects
X
such that there is a triangle
U\to X\to V\to U[1]
with
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
V\in\mathcal{V}
.
Following
[
37
, 3.1]
, we set
\mathcal{U}\diamond\mathcal{V}:=\langle\mathcal{U}\star\mathcal{V}\rangle
, and then
define
\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle_{0}:=0
and
\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle_{n+1}:=\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle_{n}\diamond\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle
for
n\geq 0
inductively. Clearly, the objects of
\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle_{n+1}
are the
direct summands
of the objects obtained by taking
(n+1)
-fold extensions of finite direct sums of shifts of objects of
\mathcal{U}
. Following
[
37
, Definition 3.1]
, the
dimension
of
\mathcal{T}
, denoted by
\dim(\mathcal{T})
, is defined to be the minimal natural number
n

such that there is an object
X\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{T}=\langle X\rangle_{n+1}
. If no such
X
exists, one defines
\dim(\mathcal{T})=\infty
. If
\dim(\mathcal{T})
is finite, then such an object
X
is called a
strong generator
of
\mathcal{T}
.

For a pair of integers
i\leq j
, we denote by
\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle_{n+1}^{[i,j]}
the full subcategory of
\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle_{n+1}
consisting of all objects which are obtained by taking
(n+1)
-fold extensions of finite direct sums of objects in the class

\{U[-s]\mid U\in\mathcal{U},s\in\mathbb{Z},i\leq s\leq j\}
. Here, we do not require taking both direct summands
and arbitrary shifts in
\langle\mathcal{U}\rangle_{n+1}^{[i,j]}
.

Corollary 4.18
.

(1)
Let
\mathcal{S}
be the finite set of isomorphism classes of simple objects of
\mathscr{H}
, and let
n
be the Loewy length of the algebra
\Gamma
.
Then
(\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
is a compactly generated triangulated category:

\big(\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S}\big)/[M]=\langle{\rm Add}(\mathcal{S})\rangle_{2n}^{[-1,0]}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\big((\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S}\big)/[M])^{\rm c}=(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=\langle\mathcal{S}\rangle_{2n}^{[-1,0]}.

(2)
If
A
is symmetric and
\Gamma
is semisimple, then there are triangle equivalences

(\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]={\rm Add}\big(N\oplus\Omega_{A}^{-}(N)\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\Gamma\times\Gamma)\mbox{{\rm-Mod}},

(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]={\rm add}\big(N\oplus\Omega_{A}^{-}(N)\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\Gamma\times\Gamma)\mbox{{\rm-mod}},

where
(\Gamma\times\Gamma)\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
, as a triangulated category, has the shift functor induced from the automorphism of the algebra

\Gamma\times\Gamma
by permutating the first and second coordinate.

(3)
If
T(X)\in\mathscr{E}/[M]
for any
X\in A\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
, then the recollement in Proposition
4.3
restricts to
a half recollement of triangulated categories:

\textstyle{(\underline{M}^{\bot})^{\rm c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\pi\circ\rm inc}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}^{\rm c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Psi}}
\textstyle{(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M].\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm inc}}

Proof.

(1)
Let
\mathscr{R}:=(\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
. By Lemma
4.2
(2)(b), we can identify
\mathscr{H}
with its (essential) image in
\mathscr{R}
under the quotient functor
\underline{\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S}}\to\mathscr{R}
. It follows from the first part of Corollary
4.8
(3) that
\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{H}[1]\star\mathscr{H}
, where
[1]
denotes the functor
\Omega_{M}^{-}
. To characterize
\mathscr{R}
in terms of
\mathcal{S}
, we use the functor
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,-):\mathscr{H}\to\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
which is an equivalence of abelian categories sending
N
to
\Gamma
, due to Lemma
4.7
(2)(3). Recall that
\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}:=\{X\in\mathscr{H}\mid{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(M,X)\in\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-mod}}\}=\mathscr{H}\cap\underline{\mathscr{E}}
. By the proof of Corollary
4.8
(1), the functor
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,-)
restricts to an equivalence:
\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
. This equivalence clearly sends simple objects of
\mathscr{H}
to simple
\Gamma
-modules. Since
\Gamma
is an Artin algebra, it has only finitely many isomorphism classes of simple modules and each (respectively, finitely generated)

\Gamma
-module is generated by simple modules under arbitrary (respectively, finite) direct sums and taking
n
-fold extensions. Consequently,
\mathcal{S}
is a finite set and each object of
\mathscr{H}
(respectively,
\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}
) is generated by
\mathcal{S}
under arbitrary (respectively, finite) direct sums and taking
n
-fold extensions. Note that each short exact sequence in
\mathscr{H}
induces a triangle in
\mathscr{R}
by Lemma
3.16
(2). Now, by
\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{H}[1]\star\mathscr{H}
, the first equality in
(1)
holds, and therefore
\mathscr{R}={\rm Loc}(\mathcal{S})
. Similarly, from the equality
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}[1]\star\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}
by the second part of Corollary
4.8
(3), we see that
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
is generated by
\mathcal{S}
under taking the shifts
[i]
for
i=0,1
and
(2n)
-fold extensions. This implies the third equality in
(1)
. By Proposition
4.17
(1), each object of
\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}
is compact in
\mathscr{C}
and thus also in
\mathscr{R}
. Hence
\mathscr{R}
is compactly generated. By
[
33
, Theorem 4.4.9]
, we have
\mathscr{R}^{\rm c}={\rm thick}(\mathcal{S})
.
Clearly,
{\rm thick}(\mathcal{S})\subseteq(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=\langle\mathcal{S}\rangle_{2n}^{[-1,0]}\subseteq{\rm thick}(\mathcal{S})
. Thus
\mathscr{R}^{\rm c}=\langle\mathcal{S}\rangle_{2n}^{[-1,0]}
.

(2)
Suppose that
A
is symmetric and
\Gamma
is semisimple. Then
\Gamma
is symmetric. By Corollary
4.11
,
\Omega_{M}^{2}(N)\simeq N
in
\mathscr{C}
.
Moreover, by Lemma
4.10
,
\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)\simeq\Omega_{A}^{-}(N)
in
\mathscr{C}
. It follows that
\Omega_{M}^{2i}(N)\simeq N
and
\Omega_{M}^{2i+1}(N)\simeq\Omega_{A}^{-}(N)
for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
W:=N\oplus\Omega_{A}^{-}(N)
and
\mathscr{W}:={\rm Add}(W)
. Then
\mathscr{W}=\Omega_{M}(\mathscr{W})
. Clearly,
\mathscr{W}
contains
N
and is closed under direct sums in
\mathscr{C}
. As
\Gamma
is semisimple, there holds
{\rm add}(N)={\rm add}(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathscr{R}
. To show
\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{W}
, it suffices to show that
\mathscr{W}
is a triangulated subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
.
Since
\mathscr{W}
is closed under
\Omega_{M}
, we only need to show that, for any morphism
f:X_{1}\to X_{2}
in
\mathscr{W}
and triangle
X_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}X_{2}\rightarrow X_{3}\rightarrow\Omega_{M}^{-}(X_{1})
in
\mathscr{C}
, the term
X_{3}
belongs to
\mathscr{W}
.

Since
\mathscr{H}
is the heart of the torsion pair
(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
,
{\rm Ext}_{\mathscr{H}}^{j}(U,V)\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(U,V[j])
for any
U,V\in\mathscr{H}
and
j=0,1
. Since
N
is a projective object in
\mathscr{H}
by Lemma
4.7
(3), we have
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,N[1])\simeq{\rm Ext}_{\mathscr{H}}^{1}(N,N)=0
. Further, by Lemma
3.17
and
[
16
, Lemma 3.5]
, if
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
lie in
\mathscr{G}
, then
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(V_{1},V_{2}[n])

\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{M}(V_{1},\Omega_{M}^{-n}(V_{2}))
for all
n\geq 1
. This implies
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,N[1])\simeq{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{M}(N,\Omega_{M}^{-}(N))
, and therefore
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{M}(N,\Omega_{M}^{-}(N))=0={\rm\underline{Hom}}_{M}(\Omega_{M}^{-}(N),N)
. Let
B:={\rm\underline{End}}_{A}(W)
. Then
B\simeq\Gamma\oplus\Gamma
as algebras.
Since
B
is semisimple and
W
is compact in
\mathscr{C}
by Lemma
4.14
(1), the functor
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{M}(W,-):\mathscr{W}\to B\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
is an additive equivalence. By this equivalence and the fact that
B\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
is a semisimple abelian category, it can be proved that
f
as a morphism in
\mathscr{W}
is isomorphic to a direct sum of the identity map of
Z_{1}
with the zero map
Z_{2}\to Z_{3}
, where
Z_{i}\in\mathscr{W}
for
1\leq i\leq 3
.
Consequently,
X_{3}\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}(Z_{2})\oplus Z_{3}
. It then follows from
\mathscr{W}=\Omega_{M}(\mathscr{W})
that
X_{3}\in\mathscr{W}
. Thus
\mathscr{W}
is a triangulated subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
, and therefore
\mathscr{R}=\mathscr{W}
.
By
(1)
,
\mathscr{R}^{\rm c}={\rm add}(W)
.

(3)
By Lemma
3.20
(4) and
[
33
, Theorem 4.4.9]
, the assumption of Corollary
4.18
(3) implies

\mathscr{C}^{\rm c}\subseteq\mathscr{E}/[M]
. Combining Corollary
4.5
with Proposition
4.17
(1), we see that
\widetilde{\Phi}
sends compact objects of
\mathscr{C}
to objects of
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]
which are also compact in
\mathscr{C}
. Note that

\widetilde{\Psi}:\mathscr{C}\to\underline{M}^{\bot}
always preserves compact generating sets. Thus the first two lines of functors in the recollement of Corollary
4.5
restrict to the half recollement in Corollary
4.18
(3).
\square

Corollary 4.19
.

(1)

\dim\big((\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M])\big)\leq 2\;LL(\Gamma)-1
, where
LL(\Gamma)
is the Loewy length of
\Gamma
.

(2)

\dim\big((\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M])\big)\leq 2\,{\rm gl.dim}(\Gamma)+1
, where
{\rm gl.dim}(\Gamma)
is the global dimension of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

(1)
follows from Corollary
4.18
(1).

(2)
If
{\rm gl.dim}(\Gamma)
is infinite, then the inequality in
(2)
holds trivially. Now, let
m:={\rm gl.dim}(\Gamma)<\infty
. In the following, we follow the notation in the proof of Corollary
4.18
(1) and show that
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=\langle{\rm add}(N)\rangle_{2m+2}^{[-m-1,0]}
. This implies

\dim\big((\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M])\big)\leq 2m+1
.

By the second part of Corollary
4.8
(3),
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}[1]\star\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}
. So, it suffices to control the objects of

\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}
by
N
. We take an object
X\in\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}
. Since
the functor
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,-):\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}\to\Gamma\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
is an equivalence of abelian categories sending
N
to
\Gamma
and since each finitely generated
\Gamma
-module has a projective resolution of length
m
by finitely generated projective
\Gamma
-modules, there is a long exact sequence

(\ast):\;0\to N_{m}\to\cdots\to N_{1}\to N_{0}\to X\to 0
in
\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}
with
N_{i}\in{\rm add}(N)
for
0\leq i\leq m
. Note that each short exact sequence

0\to X\to Y\to Z\to 0
in
\mathscr{H}
gives rise to a triangle

X\to Y\to Z\to\Omega_{A}^{-1}(X)
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
with the terms
X,Y,Z\in\mathscr{H}
.
Since
\mathscr{H}\subseteq\underline{\mathscr{G}\cap\mathscr{S}}\subseteq\underline{{{}^{\bot 1}}M}
by Lemma
4.2
(1), this triangle induces a triangle
X\to Y\to Z\to X[1]
in
\mathscr{C}
by Lemma
3.16
(2). So, we divide the sequence
(\ast)
into a series of short exact sequences in
\mathscr{H}^{\rm fg}

and then obtain
X\in\langle{\rm add}(N)\rangle_{m+1}^{[-m,0]}
.
Thus
(\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S})/[M]=\langle{\rm add}(N)\rangle_{2m+2}^{[-m-1,0]}
.
\square

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.
The existence of the recollement in
(1)
follows from Proposition
4.3
, while
(2)
and (3) are exactly Corollaries
4.5
and
4.19
, respectively.
\square

Proof of Proposition
1.6
.

(2)
and
(3)
follow from Corollary
4.18
(1), while the first part of
(1)
is Proposition
4.17
(1). Let
\mathscr{E}_{0}:=\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S}\cap A\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
. Clearly,
{\rm add}(M)\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{0}
. If
X\in\mathscr{E}_{0}
, then
X\in{\rm Add}(M)
by Proposition
4.17
(2) because finitely generated
A
-modules are pure-projective. As
X
is finitely generated, it lies in
{\rm add}(M)
. Thus
\mathscr{E}_{0}={\rm add}(M)
.
This implies the second part of
(1)
.
\square

5
Tachikawa’s second conjecture

In this section we prove Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
1.4
. As a consequence, we show that the Nakayama conjecture is true for Gorenstein-Morita algebras (see Definition
1.3
(ii)). Moreover, we introduce two homological conditions
{\rm(CI)}
and
{\rm(CII)}
). They are connected with finitistic dimension (see Lemma
5.1
). Also, the invariance of
{\rm(CII)}
under different types of equivalences between algebras is discussed in Corollary
5.3
.

Let
B
be an Artin algebra. The
dominant dimension
of
B
, denoted by

{\rm dom.dim}(B)
, is by definition the largest natural number
n
or
\infty
such that, in a minimal injective coresolution

0\to{{}_{B}}B\to I^{0}\to I^{1}\to\cdots\to I^{n}\to\cdots
,
all these module
I^{i}
are projective for
0\leq i<n
.
The unsolved Nakayama Conjecture says that an Artin algebra is self-injective whenever its dominant dimension is infinite. Related to this conjecture, Tachikawa proposed two conjectures in
[
40
, p.​ 115-116]
.

(TC1)
:
If an Artin algebra
B
satisfies
{\rm Ext}^{n}_{B}(D(B),B)=0
for all
n\geq 1
, then
B
is self-injective.

(TC2)
:
Let
B
be a self-injective algebra and
Y
a finitely
generated
B
-module. If
{\rm Ext}^{n}_{B}(Y,Y)=0
for all
n\geq 1
, then
Y
is projective.

As pointed in the introduction, the two conjectures
(TC1)
and
(TC2)
hold true for all algebras if and only if so does the Nakayam conjecture for all algebras. Moreover, it was shown in
[
31
]
that, given a pair
(B,Y)
with
B
a self-injective algebra and
Y
a finitely generated, self-orthogonal
B
-module, the algebra
{\rm End}_{B}(B\oplus Y)
satisfies the Nakayama conjecture if and only if
Y
is projective.

By
[
31
]
, algebras of dominant dimension at least
2
are exactly endomorphism algebras of
generator-cogenerators over algebras. Recall that a finitely generated module
X
over an algebra
C
is called
a
generator-cogenerator
if
C\oplus D(C)\in{\rm add}({{}_{C}}X)
.
If
B
is the endomorphism algebra of a generator-cogenerator
X
over an algebra
C
, then, by Müller’s theorem (see
[
31
, Lemma 3]
),
{\rm dom.dim}(B)=n>1
if and only if
{\rm Ext}_{C}^{i}(X,X)=0
for
1\leq i<n-1
.
In particular,
{\rm dom.dim}(B)=\infty
if and only if
X
is self-orthogonal, that is,
{\rm Ext}_{C}^{i}(X,X)=0
for all
i\geq 1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)\mbox{-}(5)
.
Suppose
{{}_{A}}M
is projective. Then
\mathscr{G}=A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
and
W=\Omega_{A}^{-}(M)=0
.
This implies
(4)
and
(5)
. Since each
A
-module is always a filtered colimit of finitely generated
A
-modules,
(2)
holds. Note that
A
-filtered modules are projective. Thus
(3)
holds.

(2)\Rightarrow(4)
. This is clear since
W\in\mathscr{G}
.

(4)\Rightarrow(5)
. Let
f:\Omega_{A}^{-}(M)\to W
be the minimal left
\mathscr{G}
-approximation of
\Omega_{A}^{-}(M)
with
W\in\mathscr{G}
. Assume that
W
is a filtered colimit of
\{W_{i}\mid i\in I\}
with
I
an essentially small, filtered category and with all
W_{i}\in\mathscr{G}_{0}
. We show that
W^{\bot 1}
is closed under countable direct sums in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. This implies
(4)
.

Let
\lambda_{i}:W_{i}\to W
be the canonical homomorphism of the colimit
W
. Since
\Omega_{A}^{-}(M)
is finitely generated and even finitely presented, the canonical map
\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow}{\rm Hom}_{A}(\Omega_{A}^{-}(M),W_{i})\to{\rm Hom}_{A}(\Omega_{A}^{-}(M),W)
induced by

\{{\rm Hom}_{A}(\Omega_{A}^{-}(M),\lambda_{i})\mid i\in I\}
is an isomorphism. As
I
is a filtered category, the colimt
\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow}{\rm Hom}_{A}(\Omega_{A}^{-}(M),W_{i})
of abelian groups is a quotient group of the direct sum
\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\rm Hom}_{A}(\Omega_{A}^{-}(M),W_{i})
and each of its elements can be represented by a homomorphism of
{\rm Hom}_{A}(\Omega_{A}^{-}(M),W_{i})
for some index
i\in I
. Consequently, there is an index
i\in I
and a homomorphism
f_{i}:\Omega_{A}^{-}(M)\to W_{i}
such that
f=f_{i}\lambda_{i}
. Further, due to
W_{i}\in\mathscr{G}
, the approximation implies that there is a homomorphism
g_{i}:W\to W_{i}
such that
f_{i}=fg_{i}
. It follows that
f=fg_{i}f_{i}
. Since
f
is left minimal,
g_{i}f_{i}
is an isomorphism. Thus
g_{i}
is split-injective. Since
W_{i}
is finitely generated,
W
is also finitely generated (presented). Hence,
W^{\bot 1}
is closed under arbitrary direct sums in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
.

(5)\Rightarrow(1)
. By Lemma
4.15
(3),
T(\Omega_{A}^{-}(M))\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)
in
\mathscr{C}
. Thus
W\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)
in
\mathscr{C}
. By Lemma
3.16
(1), there are
M_{1},M_{2}\in{\rm Add}(M)
such that
W\oplus M_{1}\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)\oplus M_{2}
. Let
\mathscr{M}
be the full subcategory of
A\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
consisting of modules with finite
{\rm add}(M)
-resolutions. By
M\in\mathscr{G}^{\bot>0}
and
W\in\mathscr{G}
, we have
\mathscr{M}\subseteq\mathscr{G}^{\bot>0}\subseteq W^{\bot 1}
. For
X\in{\rm Add}(M)
, since
{{}_{A}}M
is self-orthogonal and finitely presented,
X^{\bot 1}
contains
\mathscr{M}
and is closed under arbitrary direct sums in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. Thus
(3)
implies that
\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)^{\bot 1}
is closed under countable direct sums in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
of modules in
\mathscr{M}
. By Lemma
4.16
,
\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)^{\bot 1}
is closed under countable direct sums in
A\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
of finitely
M
-filtered
A
-modules. Further, it follows from
N\in\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S}
, Corollary
3.13
and Lemma
4.4
(2) that
\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)\in\mathscr{E}\cap\mathscr{S}
. By Proposition
4.17
(2),
\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)\in{\rm Add}(M)
. This shows
\Omega_{M}^{-}(N)=0
(and thus also
N=0
) in
\mathscr{C}
. By
{\rm\underline{End}}_{A}(N)={\rm\underline{End}}_{M}(N)
, we have
N=0
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. Since
{\rm\underline{End}}_{A}(M)\simeq{\rm\underline{End}}_{A}(N)
by Lemma
4.7
(2),
M=0
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. In other words,
{{}_{A}}M
is projective.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
. By Proposition
1.6
(1),
the module
N
is
M
-compact (that is, compact in
\mathscr{C}
)
and isomorphic in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
to an
M
-filtered module
X
. Then there are projective
A
-modules
P
and
Q
with
N\oplus P\simeq X\oplus Q
. Since projective
A
-modules are zero in
\mathscr{C}
,
X
is
M
-compact. By
(3)
,
X
lies in
{\rm Add}(M)
, and therefore
N=0
in
\mathscr{C}
. By Lemma
4.7
(2), we have
M=0
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
. Thus
{{}_{A}}M
is projective.
\square

Let
\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(B)
be the category of finitely generated
B
-modules with finite projective dimension, and let
B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}_{\omega}
be the category of
countably generated, compactly Gorenstein-projective

B
-modules. Clearly, finitely generated, Gorenstein-projective
B
-modules are in
B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}_{\omega}
. Note that the category of countably generated
B
-modules is
a Serre subcategory of
B\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. This is due to the fact: A ring
R
has the property that each submodule of each countably generated left
R
-module is countably generated if and only if each left ideal of
R
is countably generated.

We consider the following two homological conditions:

{\rm(CI)}
The direct sum of countably many
B
-modules from
\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(B)
belongs to
B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}_{\omega}^{\,\,\bot>0}
.

{\rm(CII)}
Any compactly Gorenstein-projective, compactly filtered
B
-module is projective.

The
finitistic dimension
of an Artin algebra
B
is the supremum of projective dimensions of all
B
-modules in
\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(B)
. The well-known finitistic dimension conjecture says that an Artin algebra
B
should always have finite finitistic dimension.
The validity of this conjecture for
B
implies the one of the Nakayama conjecture for
B
. However, the finitistic dimension conjecture is still open.

Lemma 5.1
.

(1)
If
{\rm(CI)}
holds, then so does
{\rm(CII)}
.

(2)
If an Artin algebra
B
has finite finitistic dimension or is a virtually Gorenstein algebra, then
{\rm(CI)}
holds.

Proof.

(1)

Let
Y
be a compactly Gorenstein-projective
B
-module which is compactly filtered by a sequence
\{Y_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
of submodules of
Y
. Set
X:=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{N}}Y_{i}
. Then there is a canonical exact sequence
0\to X\to X\to Y\to 0
in
B\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. Since
Y_{i}
are finitely generated for all
i
, the module
Y
is countably generated. This implies
Y\in B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}_{\omega}
. Moreover, since
Y_{i}\in\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(B)
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the condition (CI) implies that
the sequence splits and
Y
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
X
. Note that
Y_{i}
is a direct sum of finitely many indecomposable submodules with the local endomorphism rings. By
[
1
, Corollary 26.6]
,
Y\simeq\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{N}}Y_{i}^{\prime}
, where
Y_{i}^{\prime}
is a direct summand of
Y_{i}
for each
i
.
Since
\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(B)\cap B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}={\rm add}(B)
,
Y_{i}^{\prime}
is projective for all
i
. Thus
Y
is projective. This shows that (CII) holds.

(2)
Clearly,
B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}^{\bot>0}
contains all
B
-modules of finite projective dimension. For a virtually Gorenstein algebra
B
,
B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}^{\bot>0}={{}^{\bot>0}}B\mbox{{\rm-GInj}}
, and therefore
B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}^{\bot>0}
is closed under arbitrary direct sums in
B\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
. Thus (2) holds.

\square

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
B
and
C
be Artin algebras and
X
a finitely generated
C
-
B
-bimodule. Suppose that
{{}_{C}}X
and
X_{B}
are projective and the tensor functor
X\otimes_{B}-:B\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}\to C\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
induces a triangle equivalence
B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}\to C\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}
.
If
C
satisfies
{\rm(CII)}
, then so does
B
.

Proof.
Let
F:={{}_{C}}X\otimes_{B}-
. Since both
{{}_{C}}X
and
X_{B}
are projective, the functor
F:B\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}\to C\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
is exact and preserves projective modules.
This yields
F(\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(B))\subseteq\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(C)
. Since
F
commutes with filtered colimits, it sends compactly filtered
B
-modules
to compactly filtered
C
-modules. Further, since
F
induces a triangle equivalence
B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}\to C\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}
,
it reflects projective modules and sends compactly Gorenstein-projective
B
-modules to compactly Gorenstein-projective
C
-modules.
This implies Lemma
5.2
.
\square

Next, we point out that the condition
{\rm(CII)}
is preserved by several classes of equivalences between algebras.
For the unexplained notions below of stable equivalences of adjoint type and singular equivalences of Morita type with level,
we refer to
[
43
]
and
[
41
]
, respectively. Given a finitely generated
B
-module
N
,
we denote by
{\rm thick}({{}_{B}}N)
the smallest thick subcategory of
B\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
which contains
N
.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
B
and
C
be finite-dimensional algebras over a field. Suppose that

(a)

B
and
C
are derived equivalent, or

(b)

B
and
C
are stably equivalent of adjoint type, or

(c)

B
and
C
are singularly equivalent of Morita type with level defined by a pair of bimodules
({{}_{C}}X{{}_{B}},{{}_{B}}Y_{C})
such that

{\rm Hom}_{C}(X,C)\in{\rm thick}({{}_{B}}B\oplus D(B))
and
{\rm Hom}_{B}(Y,B)\in{\rm thick}({{}_{C}}C\oplus D(C))
.

Then
B
satisfies
{\rm(CII)}
if and only if so does
C
.

Proof.

(a)
follows from Lemma
5.2
and
[
25
, Example 4.7 and Corollary 5.4]
. The case
(b)
is a special case of
(c)
.
In the case
(c)
, the modules
{{}_{C}}X
and
X_{B}
are finitely generated and projective by the definition of singular equivalences of Morita type with level.
By Lemma
5.2
, it suffices to show that the functor
F:={{}_{C}}X\otimes_{B}-:B\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}\to C\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
induces a triangle equivalence
B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}\to C\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}
. Note that for finitely generated Gorenstein-projective modules, the triangle equivalence
B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm Gproj}\to C\mbox{-}\underline{\rm Gproj}
is known in
[
41
, Proposition 4.5]
under a stronger assumption that
{\rm Hom}_{C}(X,C)\in\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(B)
and
{\rm Hom}_{B}(Y,B)\in\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(C)
. Since our discussions involve infinitely generated Gorenstin-projective modules, we have to check whether
F
and
G
can be regraded as functors between
B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}
and
C\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}
.

Let
U\in B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}
with a complete projective resolution
P^{\bullet}
. Then
F(P^{\bullet}):=(F(P^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}})
is an exact complex of projective
C
-modules. Moreover,
{\rm Hom}^{\bullet}_{C}(F(P^{\bullet}),C)\simeq{\rm Hom}^{\bullet}_{B}(P^{\bullet},{\rm Hom}_{C}(X,C))
as complexes.
By
[
14
, Theorem 2]
,
{\rm thick}({{}_{B}}B\oplus D(B))=B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}^{\bot>0}\cap B\mbox{{\rm-mod}}
. Since each cocycle of
P^{\bullet}
lies in
B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}
and

{\rm Hom}_{C}(X,C)\in{\rm thick}({{}_{B}}B\oplus D(B))
, the complex
{\rm Hom}^{\bullet}_{B}(P^{\bullet},{\rm Hom}_{C}(X,C))
is exact. Consequently,
F(P^{\bullet})
is a compete projective resolution of
F(U)
, and therefore
F(U)\in C\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}
. In other words,
F
restricts to a functor
B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}\to C\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}
. Similarly, the functor
G:={{}_{B}}Y\otimes_{C}-:C\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}\to B\mbox{{\rm-Mod}}
also restricts to a functor
C\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}\to B\mbox{{\rm-GProj}}
.

Recall that
B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}
is a triangulated category with the shift functor
\Sigma_{B}
which is a quasi-inverse of the syzygy functor
\Omega_{B}
. This implies
Y\otimes_{C}X\otimes_{B}-\simeq\Omega^{n}_{B}(-)\simeq\Sigma_{B}^{-n}:B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}\longrightarrow B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj},
where
n
is the level of the singular equivalence in
(c)
and
\Sigma_{B}^{n}
denotes the
n
-th shift functor of
B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}
. Similarly, there are equivalences
X\otimes_{B}Y\otimes_{C}-\simeq\Omega^{n}_{C}(-)\simeq\Sigma_{C}^{-n}:C\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}\rightarrow C\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}.
Thus

F
induces a triangle equivalences
B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}\longrightarrow C\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}
with a quasi-inverse

\Sigma_{B}^{n}\circ G
:
C\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}\to B\mbox{-}\underline{\rm GProj}
.
\square

Recall from Definition
1.3
(ii) that an algebra
B
is said to be
compactly Gorenstein
if
{\rm(CII)}
holds; and
Gorenstein-Morita
if
B
is both strongly Morita and compactly Gorenstein. By Lemma
5.1
, a strongly Morita algebra is Gorenstein-Morita if it is virtually Gorenstein or has finite finitistic dimension.

Proof of Corollary
1.4
.

Suppose that
\Lambda
is a Gorenstin-Morita algebra. Then there is a self-injective algebra
A
and a finitely generated
A
-module
M
which is a generator such that
\Lambda={\rm End}_{A}(M)

and
{\rm add}(M)={\rm add}(\nu_{A}(M))
. Further, suppose
{\rm dom.dim}(\Lambda)=\infty
. Then
{{}_{A}}M
is self-orthogonal by
[
31
, Lemma 3]
.

In the following, we show that
N:=H_{*}^{0}(M)=0
in
\mathscr{C}
(see Section
4.2
). If it is the case, then
{\rm\underline{End}}_{A}(M)\simeq{\rm\underline{End}}_{A}(N)
by Lemma
4.7
(2), and therefore
M=0
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
, that is,
{{}_{A}}M
is projective. Thus
\Lambda
is Morita equivalent to
A
and must be self-injective, and therefore the first part of Corollary
1.4
is proved.

Now, let
M=A\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}M_{i}
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
M_{i}
indecomposable and non-projective for
1\leq i\leq m
.
By Lemma
4.8
(2),
N\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}H_{*}^{0}(M_{i})

in
\mathscr{C}
and
H_{*}^{0}(M_{i})
are indecomposable in
\mathscr{C}
for
1\leq i\leq m
. Clearly,
H_{*}^{0}(M_{i})
as an
A
-module does not contain non-projective direct summands in
{\rm Add}(M)
, due to
{\rm\underline{Hom}}_{A}(N,M)=0
. So we can assume that
H_{*}^{0}(M_{i})=P_{i}\oplus N_{i}
as
A
-modules, where
P_{i}
is a projective
A
-module, and
N_{i}
is either zero or an indecomposable
A
-module that does not lie in
{\rm Add}(M)
. Now, we turn to show
N_{i}=0
for all
i
. This implies that
N
is projective, and thus

N=0
in
\mathscr{C}
.

By Theorem
4.14
(1) and Proposition
4.17
(1),
N_{i}
is isomorphic in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
to an
M
-filtered
A
-module
X
. Now, suppose that
X
is filtered by a sequence
\{X_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
of submodules of
X
(see Definition
1.1
). Then
X=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}X_{n}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}X_{n}
. By Lemma
4.16
,
X_{n}\in M^{\bot>0}
and has an
{\rm add}(M)
-resolution of finite length.
Applying
G:={\rm Hom}_{A}(M,-)
to the inclusions
X_{n}\to X_{n+1}
, we obtain
G(X_{n})\in\mathscr{P}^{<\infty}(\Lambda)
and injective homomorphisms
G(X_{n})\to G(X_{n+1})
. Since
{{}_{A}}M
is finitely presented,
G
commutes with filtered colimits. It follows that
G(X)=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}G(X_{n})=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}G(X_{n})
. In other words,
G(X)
is compactly filtered.

Assume
N_{i}\neq 0
. Then
N_{i}
is indecomposable and does not belong to
{\rm Add}(M)
. It follows from
X\simeq N_{i}
in
A\mbox{{\rm-{\lx@text@underline{Mod}}}}
and
N_{i}\in\mathscr{G}
that
X\in\mathscr{G}
and
\Omega_{M}^{-}\Omega_{M}(X)\simeq\Omega_{M}^{-}\Omega_{M}(N_{i})
. By Remark
3.19
,
\Omega_{M}^{-}\Omega_{M}(N_{i})\simeq N_{i}
, and therefore
X\simeq N_{i}\oplus M_{0}
for some
M_{0}\in{\rm Add}(M)
. As
N
is compact in
\mathscr{C}
by Theorem
4.14
(1),
X
is also compact in
\mathscr{C}
. It follows from Lemma
3.17
(2) that
G(X)
is compactly Gorenstein-projective, but not projective. Since
\Lambda
is compactly Gorenstein,
G(X)
must be projective. This leads to a contradiction. Thus
N_{i}=0
.

If
\Lambda
is gendo-symmetric, then
A
can be assumed to be symmetric. Since gendo-symmetric, virtually Gorenstein algebras are Gorenstein-Morita, the second part of Corollary
1.4

follows from the first part of Corollary
1.4
.
\square

To end this section, we mention the following questions related to the results in this paper.

Question
1. Find necessary and sufficient conditions for Artin algebras to be compactly Gorenstein.

Finitely generated Gorenstein-projective modules over an Artin algebra are compactly Gorenstein-projective. The next question is about countably generated Gorenstein-projective modules.

Question
2. Describe the class of countably generated, compactly Gorenstein-projective modules over an Artin algebra.

Question
3. Find more new examples of Gorenstein-Morita algebras.
